the first part we're going to recap actually what we did in the entire class just to see how different

pieces kind of fit together. In the second part we look at some topics that are particularly trending

in 2025 and what we think are going to be trending in the near future. And then the third part will be

more a way for us to just conclude and next steps for all of you. Does that sound good?

Cool. So with that we're going to start with the first part which what I mentioned is about

recapping what we did this entire quarter. So nothing new here is just a way for us to

piece everything together. So if you remember a lot of weeks ago I believe it's like maybe

### [00:01:29.020 - 00:01:36.780]

10 weeks ago we had lecture one which was focused on understanding what transformers were.

### [00:01:36.780 - 00:01:45.200]

So at the very beginning of the class we didn't even know how we could process text. So I guess the

### [00:01:45.200 - 00:01:54.620]

first step that we saw was this tokenization step which consists of dividing the inputs into atomic units.

### [00:01:55.560 - 00:02:02.640]

And so here the way we divide the text is something that is arbitrary in some sense.

### [00:02:02.640 - 00:02:10.020]

So we have different algorithms that allow us to do that. And we saw that the most common

### [00:02:10.020 - 00:02:17.620]

tokenization algorithm is the subword level tokenizer. And we saw that some of the advantages

### [00:02:17.620 - 00:02:28.640]

were that routes of words could be reused and leveraged especially when it came to representing

### [00:02:28.640 - 00:02:39.680]

those tokens. And speaking of representation, once we were able to divide the input text into atomic units,

### [00:02:39.680 - 00:02:50.240]

aka tokens, the next step for us was to learn how to represent these embeddings. So if you remember we saw

### [00:02:50.240 - 00:02:57.920]

some methods that were very popular back then. So one of them was called Word2Vec and the representation

### [00:02:57.920 - 00:03:07.360]

was learned from a proxy task which was something like predicting the center word or predicting the context words.

### [00:03:08.960 - 00:03:14.400]

But then we saw that this way of learning representations had some limitations.

### [00:03:15.200 - 00:03:21.680]

One of which was that these representations were not context aware, meaning that

### [00:03:21.680 - 00:03:32.720]

that if a word is in a given sentence or in another sentence, they will both have the same, like that word will have the same representation in both sentences.

### [00:03:34.400 - 00:03:44.960]

And so for that reason, we saw some other methods that were popular in the 2010s, one of which was RNNs, if you remember.

### [00:03:44.960 - 00:03:56.640]

So RNNs had this recurrent structure, which processed tokens one at a time, and kept an internal representation of the sequence so far.

### [00:03:57.920 - 00:04:03.520]

But then we saw that a big limitation of this was this problem of long-range dependency,

### [00:04:04.160 - 00:04:14.240]

and in particular the fact that tokens that were encoded far in the past were not quantities that were able to be kept,

### [00:04:14.240 - 00:04:16.480]

I guess, as the sequence got longer.

### [00:04:18.080 - 00:04:25.600]

And this is the reason why we saw the central idea of this whole class, which is the idea of self-attention,

### [00:04:26.480 - 00:04:35.120]

where tokens can actually attend to one another, regardless of where they are placed in the sequence.

### [00:04:35.120 - 00:04:37.600]

So you can think of this as a direct link.

### [00:04:39.520 - 00:04:43.200]

And so this, for instance, is what we saw.

### [00:04:43.200 - 00:04:51.040]

We saw that there are like three main terminologies that people use, so query, key, and value.

### [00:04:51.680 - 00:04:59.280]

So typically you want to know how similar a query is compared to the keys in the sequence.

### [00:04:59.920 - 00:05:07.360]

And you quantify that by taking some dot products that's kind of scaled and soft maxed.

### [00:05:08.240 - 00:05:11.680]

And then you have the corresponding value that is taken.

### [00:05:11.680 - 00:05:20.080]

So at the end of the day, we obtain some kind of weighted average of all the tokens that are in the sequence.

### [00:05:22.720 - 00:05:26.240]

And then you may also be familiar now with this formula.

### [00:05:26.240 - 00:05:33.120]

So soft max of q k transpose over square root of dk times v.

### [00:05:33.120 - 00:05:44.160]

So this is the matrix formulation of what I mentioned here, which is able to process these computations in a very efficient way.

### [00:05:44.160 - 00:05:48.560]

And so it's something that today's hardware is well equipped to do.

### [00:05:50.240 - 00:06:00.400]

And then we finish the first lecture by going through the architecture that is the foundation of modern day LLMs, which is the transformer.

### [00:06:00.400 - 00:06:06.800]

And we saw that there are two notable parts in the transformer.

### [00:06:06.800 - 00:06:14.400]

So one was the encoder in the left part of the figure, and then the right part is the decoder.

### [00:06:15.040 - 00:06:18.480]

And we saw how this was applied in the case of translation.

### [00:06:20.480 - 00:06:29.360]

So at the end of the first lecture, we saw what motivated us to end up with the transformer.

### [00:06:30.960 - 00:06:35.920]

And we saw that the transformer was working quite well in the case of translation.

### [00:06:35.920 - 00:06:47.280]

And so in the next lecture, what we saw was what were the little improvements that people have made to this architecture since it was released.

### [00:06:47.280 - 00:06:50.640]

And if you remember, it was in 2017 that it was published.

### [00:06:50.640 - 00:07:02.080]

So one particular improvement that people have made is in the way we consider positions.

### [00:07:02.080 - 00:07:15.520]

Because in the original transformer paper, positions were encoded in an absolute way, as in each position had its own embedding.

### [00:07:15.520 - 00:07:20.000]

And this embedding was added to the token embedding.

### [00:07:21.680 - 00:07:29.760]

But then if we think about it, positions, actually, we don't really care about the absolute position.

### [00:07:29.760 - 00:07:33.600]

We care about the relative position between tokens.

### [00:07:34.800 - 00:07:40.560]

And in particular, we care about how far tokens are in the self-attention computation.

### [00:07:40.560 - 00:07:51.520]

Which is why we saw this method that is now quite popular called rotary position embeddings, a.k.a. rope, that is now quite used.

### [00:07:51.520 - 00:08:02.080]

And it is a method that rotates query and keys, both of which happen in the self-attention computation.

### [00:08:02.080 - 00:08:14.720]

And so here, what is quantified here is purely a function of the relative distance between two tokens.

### [00:08:15.120 - 00:08:22.800]

And not only that, it is something that is taken care of in the self-attention layer, which is what we care about.

### [00:08:22.800 - 00:08:26.480]

So this was one big improvement.

### [00:08:26.480 - 00:08:37.200]

And then we saw some other improvements, especially when it came to how the multi-head attention layer was composed of.

### [00:08:38.080 - 00:08:46.160]

And in particular, we saw that it was possible for us to have some groupings of the matrices that we learn.

### [00:08:46.160 - 00:08:55.680]

So we don't need to have one matrix, one projection matrix per head for, let's say, keys and values.

### [00:08:56.320 - 00:08:59.000]

We can actually group them.

### [00:08:59.760 - 00:09:02.160]

So this is, for instance, what is mentioned here.

### [00:09:02.260 - 00:09:03.440]

So group query attention.

### [00:09:04.880 - 00:09:09.560]

And then we also saw some other techniques that I have not represented here.

### [00:09:09.560 - 00:09:18.920]

Like, for instance, the normalization layer in the transformer, which here happens after each sub-layer.

### [00:09:18.920 - 00:09:26.040]

But I guess nowadays people have tried moving the normalization piece before the sub-layer.

### [00:09:26.040 - 00:09:29.720]

So here it's the post-norm version.

### [00:09:29.720 - 00:09:35.880]

And then the before the sub-layer part is called the pre-norm version.

### [00:09:35.880 - 00:09:47.720]

And then the last thing that we saw was that from this transformer architecture, there were a lot of derived models that were based from that.

### [00:09:47.720 - 00:09:55.880]

So we saw that if we only keep the encoder part, we could compute very meaningful embeddings.

### [00:09:55.880 - 00:10:04.600]

If you remember, there was this kind of landmark paper on encoder-only model, which is BERT,

### [00:10:04.600 - 00:10:14.120]

which was heavily used in the context of classification, because it relied on the encoded embedding of the CLS token.

### [00:10:14.120 - 00:10:14.840]

So that was one.

### [00:10:16.280 - 00:10:23.480]

But then we also saw that there was a number of other kinds of models, all more or less derived from the transformer.

### [00:10:23.480 - 00:10:26.520]

So you could only keep the encoder, which was for BERT.

### [00:10:26.520 - 00:10:30.920]

You could only keep the decoder, just for instance, for GPT.

### [00:10:31.960 - 00:10:37.640]

And you could also have both, which is for instance, the case of T5.

### [00:10:39.320 - 00:10:51.240]

And one particular aspect of each of these models is that encoder-only is not able, in the way that we saw, is not able to generate text.

### [00:10:51.240 - 00:10:56.360]

But it's able to generate embeddings, which can be used for downstream tasks.

### [00:10:57.000 - 00:11:09.160]

But then encoder-decoder models like T5 or decoder-only models like GPT, they can be auto-regressive and generate text.

### [00:11:10.120 - 00:11:13.480]

The paradigm can be text in, text out.

### [00:11:13.480 - 00:11:29.320]

And with that, we then focused on what now everyone calls large language models, which are transformer-based models, specifically text-to-text models.

### [00:11:30.120 - 00:11:33.480]

So decoder-only transformer-based models.

### [00:11:33.480 - 00:11:46.040]

And we saw that people have come up with a lot of new tricks now, because these models, as the name indicates, people have scaled them up.

### [00:11:46.600 - 00:11:59.480]

But then one question was kind of thrown, which is, do you actually need all these parameters to just do a forward pass?

### [00:11:59.480 - 00:12:08.040]

So we saw one kind of variant, which was based on a mixture of experts.

### [00:12:09.480 - 00:12:16.600]

So what mixture of experts are is, instead of running everything through the whole entire model,

### [00:12:17.640 - 00:12:25.480]

you're going to instead have a number of experts that you're going to activate in a sparse way.

### [00:12:25.480 - 00:12:34.840]

So for instance, for one input, you're going to just activate just a subset, and then for another input, you're going to activate another subset,

### [00:12:35.480 - 00:12:39.080]

so that you don't need to do all the computations all the time.

### [00:12:40.920 - 00:12:50.120]

And we saw that this mixture of experts, they were used in LLMs, in particular in the feed-foral neural network layer.

### [00:12:50.120 - 00:12:59.480]

So here you would have experts as being different feed-foral neural networks, and you would have a gating mechanism

### [00:13:00.120 - 00:13:04.600]

that would reroute to the correct feed-foral neural network.

### [00:13:07.240 - 00:13:14.120]

And then we also saw that some papers were also able to produce some nice visualization,

### [00:13:14.120 - 00:13:22.600]

in terms of, I guess, which token gets routed to which experts, because this rerouting, we saw that it was done at the token level.

### [00:13:24.680 - 00:13:34.200]

And so one reason why it's done at the token level is to be able to, I guess, smartly put the experts on different pieces of hardware, different GPUs,

### [00:13:34.200 - 00:13:37.720]

and then kind of parallelize the computation a little bit more.

### [00:13:40.840 - 00:13:49.080]

And then we also saw that these LLMs, they always are tasked with predicting the next token.

### [00:13:49.880 - 00:13:57.080]

And in order to predict the next token, we were interested in, I guess, how we were, you know, doing this.

### [00:13:57.080 - 00:14:01.960]

And so one particular method that people use is just sample.

### [00:14:03.320 - 00:14:04.920]

Sample from the output distribution.

### [00:14:04.920 - 00:14:13.720]

So you have, let's say, given an input, you have a distribution of probabilities of what the next token would be,

### [00:14:13.720 - 00:14:15.640]

that is output by the model.

### [00:14:16.280 - 00:14:23.240]

And what you do is, instead of, let's say, taking the highest probability, which is called a greedy decoding,

### [00:14:23.240 - 00:14:27.880]

greedy kind of decoding, you actually sample.

### [00:14:29.960 - 00:14:38.920]

So it introduces some randomness and allows the model to produce a kind of a bigger variety of kinds of outputs.

### [00:14:39.480 - 00:14:50.920]

And we saw that you could adjust how much, I guess, variety you want in your outputs by tweaking a hyperparameter called temperature.

### [00:14:50.920 - 00:14:57.800]

So very low temperature leads to very spiky distributions, so more deterministic outputs.

### [00:14:58.520 - 00:15:03.960]

And higher temperatures are, I guess, a bit more random, a bit more creative.

### [00:15:06.920 - 00:15:13.800]

Okay, so until then, we saw what LLMs were, how they were based on the transformer,

### [00:15:13.800 - 00:15:16.960]

how they connected to the architecture that we saw in the first lecture.

### [00:15:16.960 - 00:15:24.880]

And then in lecture four, we saw how people actually trained those LLMs.

### [00:15:26.160 - 00:15:29.280]

Because as I mentioned, these LLMs are large.

### [00:15:30.080 - 00:15:35.280]

And so you cannot kind of naively fit them in your hardware.

### [00:15:35.280 - 00:15:37.120]

You need to be a little bit smart about it.

### [00:15:37.120 - 00:15:49.520]

So in particular, what people have kind of noticed in the early 2020s is that the bigger your model is, the better your performance.

### [00:15:51.120 - 00:15:54.800]

So people just started building bigger and bigger models.

### [00:15:54.800 - 00:16:01.040]

So here in the illustration, we saw that, so on the y-axis is the test loss.

### [00:16:01.760 - 00:16:02.960]

So the lower, the better.

### [00:16:04.000 - 00:16:12.160]

So we saw that the more compute you use, the better your test performance.

### [00:16:12.160 - 00:16:18.960]

And same with increasing the dataset size, and same with increasing the number of parameters.

### [00:16:20.960 - 00:16:24.240]

But then, as you know, compute is not infinite.

### [00:16:25.040 - 00:16:30.000]

So there was a natural question that came out of the community, which was,

### [00:16:30.000 - 00:16:48.800]

okay, if we give you a given budget, a given compute budget, can you choose, I guess, some quote-unquote optimal number of parameters and dataset size on which you want to train your model?

### [00:16:48.800 - 00:17:10.440]

And so we saw that there was this paper that was published in the early 2020s, which actually studied the relationship between, I guess, if you vary the dataset size and the size of your model, and the performance on the test set.

### [00:17:10.440 - 00:17:22.580]

And then we saw that, actually, most models at the time were, what we say, under-trained, because they were too big compared to the dataset that they were trained on.

### [00:17:22.860 - 00:17:26.480]

Like, the dataset that they were trained on, it was not as big as they should have been.

### [00:17:28.020 - 00:17:32.140]

And so in particular, there was kind of a rule of thumb that came out of this, which was,

### [00:17:32.140 - 00:17:44.180]

if you have a given number of parameters in your model, you should at least train it on 20 times the number of parameters in terms of tokens.

### [00:17:45.680 - 00:17:54.480]

So, for instance, if you have a 100 billion parameter model, you should train it on at least 2 trillion tokens.

### [00:17:55.380 - 00:17:58.720]

Because 2 trillion is 100 billion times 20.

### [00:17:58.720 - 00:18:03.200]

So, that's kind of the rule of thumb that people have used.

### [00:18:04.360 - 00:18:08.100]

And then, as I mentioned previously, these models are huge.

### [00:18:08.820 - 00:18:13.480]

So, people have tried to also make the computation more efficient.

### [00:18:15.300 - 00:18:21.520]

And so there was this method that we saw, which is actually quite important, called flash attention.

### [00:18:21.520 - 00:18:30.700]

And flash attention is a method that leverages the strength of the underlying hardware.

### [00:18:31.980 - 00:18:41.120]

And in particular, it looks at, so GPUs more particularly, it looks at the kinds of memories that a GPU has.

### [00:18:41.120 - 00:18:49.480]

So, it has a big but slow memory and a small but fast memory.

### [00:18:49.620 - 00:18:52.420]

So, the HPM and the SRAM, respectively.

### [00:18:53.060 - 00:19:05.020]

And we saw that this method tries to minimize the number of reads and writes to the big and slow memory, to the HPM.

### [00:19:05.020 - 00:19:19.720]

And so, the way it was doing this was to divide the computation in little bits that it would send to the SRAM, which is the small but fast memory.

### [00:19:19.720 - 00:19:29.340]

So, that it can do the end-to-end computation and then send it back to where it was in order to do the full end-to-end computation.

### [00:19:31.440 - 00:19:38.000]

So, that method is an exact method, meaning that we're not doing any approximations to the results.

### [00:19:38.000 - 00:19:41.400]

But it led to significant speed-ups.

### [00:19:42.240 - 00:19:48.040]

And in particular, there was this second idea from the paper, which is kind of an important one as well,

### [00:19:48.120 - 00:19:55.260]

which was that sometimes it's okay for you to not store results.

### [00:19:56.260 - 00:20:01.440]

It's okay for you to just throw them out and then recompute when you need them again.

### [00:20:01.440 - 00:20:13.880]

So, there is this idea of recomputation using what I described, which led to faster runtimes, even though we were doing more computations.

### [00:20:15.880 - 00:20:17.880]

So, that's a flash attention.

### [00:20:18.660 - 00:20:27.680]

And we also saw a number of other methods that were meant to, I guess, parallelize the computation.

### [00:20:27.680 - 00:20:37.620]

So, we saw data parallelism, which was this idea of not having all your data be processed on a single GPU,

### [00:20:37.820 - 00:20:41.840]

but instead divided into kind of multiple places.

### [00:20:43.240 - 00:20:47.760]

And then we had the second method, which was model parallelism,

### [00:20:48.960 - 00:20:54.760]

where even for a given forward pass, you would actually involve multiple GPUs.

### [00:20:54.760 - 00:20:59.680]

So, anyway, there were a lot of very interesting techniques,

### [00:20:59.880 - 00:21:06.000]

a lot of different ideas about how to train this model in an efficient way.

### [00:21:08.120 - 00:21:17.820]

And in particular, so what I described here is mostly important for the first step of the training process of an LLM,

### [00:21:17.820 - 00:21:26.500]

which is called the pre-training, which is meant to teach the model about the structure of language,

### [00:21:27.380 - 00:21:28.560]

about the structure of code.

### [00:21:29.620 - 00:21:33.780]

And in particular, this model was trained with huge amounts of data.

### [00:21:34.160 - 00:21:39.520]

So, think about trillions of tokens, or even tens of trillions of tokens.

### [00:21:39.520 - 00:21:49.400]

And so, that first step goes from an initialized model to a model that is able to autocomplete,

### [00:21:49.580 - 00:21:54.300]

because it is trained with an objective of predicting the next token.

### [00:21:54.300 - 00:22:02.900]

So, at the end of this first stage, you have a model that knows how to autocomplete,

### [00:22:03.020 - 00:22:05.140]

but you have a model that is not very helpful,

### [00:22:06.080 - 00:22:09.640]

because it only knows how to complete things.

### [00:22:11.080 - 00:22:16.480]

So, in order to have the model be useful for our use cases,

### [00:22:16.480 - 00:22:20.820]

we had this second step, which is called the fine-tuning step,

### [00:22:21.600 - 00:22:29.320]

where we teach the model on the kinds of input-output pairs that we wanted to perform well.

### [00:22:30.380 - 00:22:35.840]

So, this is also called the SFT stage, supervised fine-tuning stage.

### [00:22:36.820 - 00:22:39.020]

And at the end of this second step,

### [00:22:39.020 - 00:22:45.880]

we have a model that not only knows the structure of text and code,

### [00:22:46.060 - 00:22:48.820]

but also is able to behave in the way you want.

### [00:22:50.640 - 00:22:53.820]

But so far, up until step number two,

### [00:22:54.600 - 00:22:59.280]

we have only taught our model what to do,

### [00:22:59.740 - 00:23:02.300]

we have not taught it what to not do.

### [00:23:03.540 - 00:23:05.720]

And this is why we had our third step,

### [00:23:05.720 - 00:23:08.100]

which was the preference-tuning step.

### [00:23:09.020 - 00:23:13.180]

Where we took our model that went to the pre-training stage,

### [00:23:13.260 - 00:23:14.760]

that went to the SFT stage,

### [00:23:14.760 - 00:23:19.400]

and now we want to inject some negative signal as well,

### [00:23:19.620 - 00:23:25.260]

as in, I want you to prefer this compared to this output.

### [00:23:26.600 - 00:23:31.580]

And this third step uses preference data,

### [00:23:31.860 - 00:23:34.580]

so like the name suggests,

### [00:23:34.580 - 00:23:36.520]

so preference-tuning uses preference data,

### [00:23:36.520 - 00:23:39.340]

which is typically pairwise data,

### [00:23:39.480 - 00:23:41.060]

where humans say,

### [00:23:41.200 - 00:23:43.520]

okay, I prefer this output compared to that output.

### [00:23:45.980 - 00:23:49.640]

And typically, the model here is able to align

### [00:23:49.640 - 00:23:53.280]

the kind of output it produces with human preferences

### [00:23:53.280 - 00:23:57.260]

that could be along the dimension of usefulness,

### [00:23:57.260 - 00:24:00.500]

of safety, friendliness, tone.

### [00:24:01.320 - 00:24:02.860]

There's a bunch of different dimensions.

### [00:24:03.380 - 00:24:06.380]

But yeah, so that's what is happening in this third step.

### [00:24:08.320 - 00:24:09.840]

And in this third step,

### [00:24:10.500 - 00:24:12.260]

it's actually in lecture five

### [00:24:12.260 - 00:24:16.920]

that we dug into what that third step was about.

### [00:24:16.920 - 00:24:19.020]

So if you remember,

### [00:24:19.880 - 00:24:21.380]

we had drawn a parallel

### [00:24:21.380 - 00:24:26.520]

between the way our LLM produces tokens

### [00:24:26.520 - 00:24:29.100]

and, I guess,

### [00:24:29.340 - 00:24:31.720]

what people in the reinforcement learning fields,

### [00:24:32.300 - 00:24:33.780]

I guess,

### [00:24:34.060 - 00:24:37.180]

consider how a given policy

### [00:24:37.180 - 00:24:40.320]

is interacting with some environments

### [00:24:40.320 - 00:24:42.200]

and performing some action

### [00:24:42.200 - 00:24:43.280]

and being in some states.

### [00:24:43.280 - 00:24:47.020]

And the reason why we drew that parallel

### [00:24:47.020 - 00:24:49.240]

was to be able to leverage

### [00:24:49.240 - 00:24:51.660]

some RL-based techniques

### [00:24:51.660 - 00:24:54.100]

in order to train our model.

### [00:24:54.900 - 00:24:55.600]

So in this case,

### [00:24:55.720 - 00:24:58.800]

we said our LLM is a little bit like a policy.

### [00:25:00.020 - 00:25:01.560]

So given some states,

### [00:25:02.100 - 00:25:04.580]

which is the input it has received so far,

### [00:25:05.220 - 00:25:08.360]

it can perform the next action.

### [00:25:08.700 - 00:25:09.440]

And in this case,

### [00:25:09.580 - 00:25:11.960]

it is to predict the next token.

### [00:25:11.960 - 00:25:16.140]

And this prediction is made

### [00:25:16.140 - 00:25:18.400]

in the environment of tokens.

### [00:25:20.300 - 00:25:24.560]

And when we predict a completion,

### [00:25:25.080 - 00:25:26.740]

what we do is at the end of the day,

### [00:25:26.860 - 00:25:28.600]

we have some signal,

### [00:25:28.740 - 00:25:29.380]

some reward,

### [00:25:30.240 - 00:25:32.220]

which can be the human preference.

### [00:25:34.260 - 00:25:36.180]

So this is the parallel we drew

### [00:25:36.180 - 00:25:37.480]

with the RL worlds.

### [00:25:38.100 - 00:25:39.220]

And with that in mind,

### [00:25:39.360 - 00:25:40.700]

we talked about rewards.

### [00:25:40.700 - 00:25:42.640]

But the problem is

### [00:25:42.640 - 00:25:44.580]

that rewards are only available

### [00:25:44.580 - 00:25:46.340]

for a limited set of data,

### [00:25:47.340 - 00:25:49.220]

which is why we saw

### [00:25:49.220 - 00:25:51.580]

how to model rewards.

### [00:25:52.540 - 00:25:53.760]

So we saw this formula.

### [00:25:54.120 - 00:25:54.620]

If you remember,

### [00:25:54.800 - 00:25:57.320]

it's called the Bradley-Terry formulation,

### [00:25:58.560 - 00:26:00.860]

which models

### [00:26:00.860 - 00:26:03.640]

how the probability

### [00:26:03.640 - 00:26:06.160]

of an output being better

### [00:26:06.160 - 00:26:07.020]

than another one

### [00:26:07.020 - 00:26:09.860]

is as a function of,

### [00:26:10.300 - 00:26:10.580]

I guess,

### [00:26:10.640 - 00:26:11.280]

two scores,

### [00:26:11.400 - 00:26:13.240]

like the score of output i

### [00:26:13.240 - 00:26:14.540]

and the score of output j.

### [00:26:15.660 - 00:26:17.780]

And we saw that reward models,

### [00:26:18.240 - 00:26:20.640]

they are typically trained

### [00:26:20.640 - 00:26:22.880]

by having this formulation in mind

### [00:26:22.880 - 00:26:24.380]

in a pairwise fashion.

### [00:26:24.380 - 00:26:27.260]

So what this means is

### [00:26:27.260 - 00:26:28.680]

a reward model,

### [00:26:28.960 - 00:26:30.420]

you give it two outputs,

### [00:26:30.820 - 00:26:31.160]

you say,

### [00:26:31.220 - 00:26:31.940]

this one is good,

### [00:26:32.460 - 00:26:33.480]

this one is bad,

### [00:26:33.960 - 00:26:35.120]

and then I want you to say

### [00:26:35.120 - 00:26:35.840]

this one is good.

### [00:26:36.700 - 00:26:38.640]

You train it in a pairwise fashion,

### [00:26:39.320 - 00:26:40.540]

but then your model

### [00:26:40.540 - 00:26:41.620]

is actually predicting

### [00:26:41.620 - 00:26:43.100]

always two scores.

### [00:26:43.220 - 00:26:44.140]

It's always predicting

### [00:26:44.140 - 00:26:46.520]

the score ri for output i,

### [00:26:47.180 - 00:26:48.720]

rj for output j.

### [00:26:48.720 - 00:26:51.920]

And so at inference time,

### [00:26:52.120 - 00:26:53.860]

you're only giving it

### [00:26:53.860 - 00:26:55.100]

one output.

### [00:26:56.680 - 00:26:57.860]

So I think that's like

### [00:26:57.860 - 00:26:58.780]

one subtlety,

### [00:26:59.000 - 00:26:59.780]

like we train it

### [00:26:59.780 - 00:27:00.620]

in a pairwise way,

### [00:27:00.720 - 00:27:01.640]

but at inference time,

### [00:27:01.740 - 00:27:04.040]

we're kind of using it

### [00:27:04.040 - 00:27:06.940]

in an individual way,

### [00:27:07.000 - 00:27:07.580]

if that makes sense.

### [00:27:09.240 - 00:27:11.020]

And so once we trained

### [00:27:11.020 - 00:27:12.280]

our reward model

### [00:27:12.280 - 00:27:13.580]

using this formulation,

### [00:27:14.220 - 00:27:15.960]

then we were able

### [00:27:15.960 - 00:27:16.980]

to use it

### [00:27:16.980 - 00:27:19.660]

to steer our LLM

### [00:27:19.660 - 00:27:21.220]

towards the direction

### [00:27:21.220 - 00:27:22.220]

that we care about.

### [00:27:23.440 - 00:27:24.240]

So if you remember,

### [00:27:24.840 - 00:27:27.180]

the way we steer our LLM

### [00:27:27.180 - 00:27:28.580]

in the direction

### [00:27:28.580 - 00:27:30.320]

of human preferences

### [00:27:30.320 - 00:27:33.360]

is to give it a prompt

### [00:27:33.360 - 00:27:35.980]

so that it can produce

### [00:27:35.980 - 00:27:37.360]

a completion,

### [00:27:38.480 - 00:27:39.760]

aka a rollout,

### [00:27:40.360 - 00:27:41.860]

or in simpler terms,

### [00:27:42.260 - 00:27:43.360]

an answer.

### [00:27:43.360 - 00:27:46.140]

And then we take

### [00:27:46.140 - 00:27:46.840]

this prompt,

### [00:27:47.000 - 00:27:48.060]

we take this answer,

### [00:27:48.380 - 00:27:49.400]

we put them both

### [00:27:49.400 - 00:27:50.500]

in the reward model

### [00:27:50.500 - 00:27:51.980]

that tells us

### [00:27:51.980 - 00:27:53.020]

how good

### [00:27:53.020 - 00:27:54.340]

the model response is.

### [00:27:55.480 - 00:27:56.400]

And depending on

### [00:27:56.400 - 00:27:57.660]

what the reward model says,

### [00:27:58.340 - 00:27:59.300]

we can tune

### [00:27:59.300 - 00:28:00.820]

the weights

### [00:28:00.820 - 00:28:01.640]

of the LLM

### [00:28:01.640 - 00:28:03.960]

in a way that maximizes

### [00:28:03.960 - 00:28:04.720]

human,

### [00:28:04.920 - 00:28:05.800]

or the reward

### [00:28:05.800 - 00:28:06.340]

that we saw,

### [00:28:06.440 - 00:28:07.560]

which is trained

### [00:28:07.560 - 00:28:08.380]

on human preferences.

### [00:28:08.380 - 00:28:12.260]

And the loss function

### [00:28:12.260 - 00:28:16.860]

of this RL setup

### [00:28:16.860 - 00:28:19.460]

is typically something

### [00:28:19.460 - 00:28:22.300]

that tries to maximize rewards,

### [00:28:23.160 - 00:28:25.660]

but also keep the model

### [00:28:25.660 - 00:28:28.180]

close to the base model.

### [00:28:28.500 - 00:28:29.620]

And here by base model,

### [00:28:29.760 - 00:28:31.040]

we mean the SFT model.

### [00:28:32.060 - 00:28:33.900]

And the reason why we want that

### [00:28:33.900 - 00:28:35.720]

is because this reward

### [00:28:35.720 - 00:28:36.540]

is imperfect.

### [00:28:36.540 - 00:28:39.380]

So we saw this phenomenon

### [00:28:39.380 - 00:28:40.580]

of reward hacking,

### [00:28:41.300 - 00:28:43.260]

where your reward

### [00:28:43.260 - 00:28:44.200]

can be imperfect,

### [00:28:44.780 - 00:28:45.920]

and the LLM

### [00:28:45.920 - 00:28:46.940]

can exploit

### [00:28:46.940 - 00:28:49.520]

its imperfect nature

### [00:28:49.520 - 00:28:50.880]

to tune it

### [00:28:50.880 - 00:28:51.320]

in a way

### [00:28:51.320 - 00:28:51.840]

that actually

### [00:28:51.840 - 00:28:52.700]

does not align

### [00:28:52.700 - 00:28:53.960]

with what you want it to be.

### [00:28:54.740 - 00:28:56.200]

So you want the LLM

### [00:28:56.200 - 00:28:57.580]

to not be too far

### [00:28:57.580 - 00:28:59.380]

from the base model,

### [00:28:59.520 - 00:29:00.860]

which is actually

### [00:29:00.860 - 00:29:01.800]

already a good model.

### [00:29:02.260 - 00:29:03.240]

So it's a way

### [00:29:03.240 - 00:29:04.320]

to regularize that

### [00:29:04.320 - 00:29:04.880]

if you want.

### [00:29:04.880 - 00:29:07.600]

And you also want

### [00:29:07.600 - 00:29:11.340]

the iteration updates

### [00:29:11.340 - 00:29:13.940]

to not be too big either.

### [00:29:15.100 - 00:29:16.160]

So you typically have

### [00:29:16.160 - 00:29:17.600]

these two constraints.

### [00:29:17.700 - 00:29:18.260]

You don't want it

### [00:29:18.260 - 00:29:19.100]

to deviate too much

### [00:29:19.100 - 00:29:19.900]

from the base model,

### [00:29:20.000 - 00:29:20.720]

but you don't want it

### [00:29:20.720 - 00:29:21.460]

to deviate too much

### [00:29:21.460 - 00:29:22.200]

from the previous

### [00:29:22.200 - 00:29:23.820]

RL iteration.

### [00:29:25.560 - 00:29:26.640]

And then just as a reminder,

### [00:29:26.880 - 00:29:27.540]

I think this was

### [00:29:27.540 - 00:29:28.300]

lecture five,

### [00:29:28.380 - 00:29:29.640]

I think was the most

### [00:29:29.640 - 00:29:31.180]

technically challenging

### [00:29:31.180 - 00:29:33.400]

of the whole class.

### [00:29:33.400 - 00:29:34.600]

So completely fine

### [00:29:34.600 - 00:29:35.320]

if the first time

### [00:29:35.320 - 00:29:35.760]

you were like,

### [00:29:35.820 - 00:29:36.040]

you know,

### [00:29:36.040 - 00:29:36.820]

what's happening?

### [00:29:37.700 - 00:29:38.680]

But hopefully now

### [00:29:38.680 - 00:29:39.260]

it should be

### [00:29:39.260 - 00:29:40.600]

### [00:29:42.880 - 00:29:43.440]

Cool.

And then after lecture five,

okay,

we've done a lot

of hard work.

So the good thing

### [00:29:49.400 - 00:29:50.040]

is, you know,

### [00:29:50.080 - 00:29:50.840]

we're in 2025

### [00:29:50.840 - 00:29:54.240]

and in the past 12 months

### [00:29:54.240 - 00:29:56.140]

or now 14 months,

### [00:29:56.640 - 00:29:57.960]

we've seen a lot

### [00:29:57.960 - 00:29:58.480]

of models

### [00:29:58.480 - 00:30:00.180]

that were being released

### [00:30:00.180 - 00:30:02.000]

with these reasoning capabilities

### [00:30:02.000 - 00:30:04.100]

and the way

### [00:30:04.100 - 00:30:05.600]

they were trained

### [00:30:05.600 - 00:30:07.120]

to exhibit

### [00:30:07.120 - 00:30:08.640]

these advanced

### [00:30:08.640 - 00:30:09.660]

reasoning capabilities

### [00:30:09.660 - 00:30:11.880]

was actually leveraging

### [00:30:11.880 - 00:30:13.400]

a lot of the techniques

### [00:30:13.400 - 00:30:14.040]

that we saw

### [00:30:14.040 - 00:30:14.880]

in lecture five,

### [00:30:16.180 - 00:30:18.000]

just like RL-based techniques.

### [00:30:19.120 - 00:30:20.080]

And in particular,

### [00:30:20.420 - 00:30:21.280]

what we want

### [00:30:21.280 - 00:30:22.140]

our LLM to do

### [00:30:22.140 - 00:30:25.220]

is to output

### [00:30:25.220 - 00:30:27.120]

a reasoning chain

### [00:30:27.120 - 00:30:29.140]

before producing

### [00:30:29.140 - 00:30:30.000]

the final answer.

### [00:30:31.740 - 00:30:32.620]

And the reason

### [00:30:32.620 - 00:30:33.240]

why we wanted

### [00:30:33.240 - 00:30:33.820]

to do that

### [00:30:33.820 - 00:30:34.420]

is because

### [00:30:34.420 - 00:30:35.480]

people have seen

### [00:30:35.480 - 00:30:36.760]

that it improves

### [00:30:36.760 - 00:30:38.020]

the performance

### [00:30:38.020 - 00:30:38.580]

of the model

### [00:30:38.580 - 00:30:40.240]

and so it's actually

### [00:30:40.240 - 00:30:41.480]

relying on this idea

### [00:30:41.480 - 00:30:43.240]

of chain of thoughts

### [00:30:43.240 - 00:30:44.860]

which I believe

### [00:30:44.860 - 00:30:45.400]

we saw

### [00:30:45.400 - 00:30:46.200]

in lecture three

### [00:30:46.200 - 00:30:49.000]

which is a prompting technique

### [00:30:49.000 - 00:30:50.200]

to have your model

### [00:30:50.200 - 00:30:51.680]

output the reasoning

### [00:30:51.680 - 00:30:53.600]

before outputting

### [00:30:53.600 - 00:30:54.440]

the response.

### [00:30:56.000 - 00:30:57.500]

So long story short,

### [00:30:58.060 - 00:30:59.320]

up until lecture six,

### [00:31:00.060 - 00:31:01.200]

our LLM was having

### [00:31:01.200 - 00:31:02.340]

a prompt as input

### [00:31:02.340 - 00:31:04.000]

directly outputting

### [00:31:04.000 - 00:31:04.500]

the output

### [00:31:04.500 - 00:31:06.500]

but in lecture seven

### [00:31:06.500 - 00:31:06.940]

we said,

### [00:31:07.780 - 00:31:08.040]

sorry,

### [00:31:08.160 - 00:31:08.740]

in lecture six

### [00:31:08.740 - 00:31:09.160]

we said,

### [00:31:09.960 - 00:31:10.880]

well,

### [00:31:11.260 - 00:31:12.320]

let's have our LLM

### [00:31:12.320 - 00:31:13.820]

actually first

### [00:31:13.820 - 00:31:15.660]

output a reasoning chain

### [00:31:15.660 - 00:31:16.700]

that the user

### [00:31:16.700 - 00:31:17.760]

### [00:31:17.760 - 00:31:18.760]

### [00:31:18.760 - 00:31:20.300]

before outputting

### [00:31:20.300 - 00:31:21.020]

### [00:31:21.020 - 00:31:25.060]

so you want

### [00:31:25.060 - 00:31:26.000]

to teach the LLM

### [00:31:26.000 - 00:31:26.460]

to do that

### [00:31:26.460 - 00:31:27.380]

so how do you do that?

### [00:31:27.900 - 00:31:28.220]

Well,

### [00:31:28.420 - 00:31:29.700]

first before doing this

I just want to show you

### [00:31:38.860 - 00:31:40.340]

to produce

### [00:31:40.340 - 00:31:41.920]

### [00:31:41.920 - 00:31:43.860]

so people have

### [00:31:43.860 - 00:31:45.020]

typically measured

### [00:31:45.020 - 00:31:46.420]

the improvement

by comparing it

I guess

### [00:31:50.580 - 00:31:51.460]

### [00:31:51.460 - 00:31:52.100]

and this one

### [00:31:53.020 - 00:31:53.560]

### [00:31:53.560 - 00:31:55.000]

benchmark

### [00:31:56.200 - 00:31:57.400]

benchmark

number of

I guess

what the LLM

outputs

### [00:32:06.400 - 00:32:07.240]

but back to

what I was

### [00:32:09.580 - 00:32:10.400]

### [00:32:10.400 - 00:32:12.040]

### [00:32:13.080 - 00:32:14.880]
